    ---
    name: drawio
    description: Always use when user asks to create, generate, draw, or design a diagram, flowchart, architecture diagram, ER diagram, sequence diagram, class diagram, network diagram, mockup, wireframe, or UI sketch, or mentions draw.io, drawio, drawoi, .drawio files, or diagram export to PNG/SVG/PDF.
    ---
    
    # Draw.io Diagram Skill
    
    Generate draw.io diagrams as native `.drawio` files. Author each diagram either as **Mermaid** (concise text that the draw.io desktop CLI converts and lays out for you) or as **draw.io XML** directly. Optionally auto-layout XML-authored diagrams with **ELK**, export to PNG/SVG/PDF with the diagram XML embedded (so the exported file stays editable in draw.io), or generate a browser URL that opens the diagram directly in the draw.io editor.
    
    ## Authoring: Mermaid or XML?
    
    The desktop CLI can convert Mermaid to a native `.drawio` file, so **prefer Mermaid** for the diagram types it handles well â€” its parser lays the diagram out automatically, which is far more reliable than hand-positioning cells in XML.
    
    | Author as | Best for | Needs desktop CLI? |
    |-----------|----------|--------------------|
    | **Mermaid** | Flowcharts, sequence, class, state, ER, gantt, mindmap, timeline, user journey, quadrant, C4, git graph, pie, and other standard types | Yes â€” to convert to `.drawio` |
    | **XML** | Custom styling, precise/hand positioning, specific shape libraries (AWS, Azure, network, UML detailâ€¦), or when the desktop CLI is not installed | No (optional ELK `--layout` needs the CLI) |
    
    - **Prefer Mermaid** when the desktop CLI is available and the request is one of the standard types above â€” write terse Mermaid and let draw.io lay it out.
    - **Use XML** for precise control, or as the universal fallback: XML needs no CLI at all, so it's the only option when the desktop app isn't installed (output a `.drawio` file or a `url`).
    - For XML-authored diagrams you can ask the CLI to apply an **ELK auto-layout** (`--layout`) instead of computing coordinates yourself â€” the same layouts the draw.io editor's *Arrange â–¸ Layout* menu applies, and the same engine the draw.io MCP app server uses. See [ELK layout for XML](#elk-layout-for-xml).
    
    If you're unsure whether the desktop CLI is present, detect it first (see [Locating the CLI](#locating-the-cli)). No CLI â†’ author as XML and deliver a `.drawio` file or a `url`.
    
    ## The pipeline
    
    Every diagram becomes a native `.drawio` file first, then is delivered in the requested output format. This keeps the delivery step identical whether you authored Mermaid or XML.
    
    1. **Author â†’ `.drawio`**
       - **Mermaid**: write the Mermaid to a `.mmd` file, then convert it with the CLI:
         ```bash
         drawio -x -f xml -o diagram.drawio diagram.mmd
         ```
         Delete the `.mmd` afterward â€” the `.drawio` is the artifact. draw.io's Mermaid parser has already laid the diagram out, so no `--layout` is needed.
       - **XML**: write the mxGraphModel XML to `diagram.drawio` (see [XML format](#xml-format)). Optionally apply an ELK layout (see [ELK layout for XML](#elk-layout-for-xml)).
    2. **Deliver** (identical for both sources):
       - *(no format)* â†’ keep `diagram.drawio` and open it.
       - **png / svg / pdf** â†’ export from the `.drawio` with embedded XML, then delete the source `.drawio`:
         ```bash
         drawio -x -f png -e -b 10 -o diagram.drawio.png diagram.drawio
         ```
       - **url** â†’ build a browser URL from the `.drawio` XML, open it, and keep the `.drawio` as a local copy (see [Browser URL output](#browser-url-output)).
    3. **Open the result** â€” the exported file, the URL, or the `.drawio`. If the open command fails, print the absolute path (or URL) so the user can open it manually.
    
    **Always convert Mermaid to `.drawio` first, then export** â€” do not export a `.mmd` straight to an image. Direct Mermaid â†’ PNG export with `-e` is broken in current draw.io Desktop (the embedded-XML step crashes); the two-step path (convert, then export the `.drawio`) is reliable and produces an editable embed. See [Troubleshooting](#troubleshooting).
    
    If Mermaid was requested but no desktop CLI is available, fall back to authoring the same diagram directly as XML.
    
    ## ELK layout for XML
    
    XML-authored diagrams can be auto-positioned by the CLI's `--layout` pass â€” the same ELK layouts as the editor's *Arrange â–¸ Layout* menu and the same engine the draw.io MCP app server uses. Generate the cells with approximate (or even `0,0`) positions and let ELK place them; you only have to get the graph *structure* â€” nodes and edges â€” right.
    
    Add `--layout <name>` to any CLI call that reads your XML. The simplest form lays out in place after you write the file (reading and overwriting the same path is supported):
    
    ```bash
    drawio -x -f xml --layout verticalFlow -o diagram.drawio diagram.drawio
    ```
    
    Or combine layout with export in a single call (works for XML input):
    
    ```bash
    drawio -x -f png -e -b 10 --layout verticalFlow -o diagram.drawio.png diagram.drawio
    ```
    
    ### Layout presets
    
    | Name | Layout |
    |------|--------|
    | `verticalFlow` | Layered, top-to-bottom â€” flowcharts, pipelines |
    | `horizontalFlow` | Layered, left-to-right |
    | `verticalTree` | Tree, top-down â€” hierarchies, org charts |
    | `horizontalTree` | Tree, left-to-right |
    | `radialTree` | Radial tree |
    | `organic` | Force-directed â€” networks, mind-map-like graphs |
    
    ### Custom layout JSON
    
    For finer control, pass a JSON **array** (starting with `[`) instead of a preset name â€” the same format as the editor's custom-layout dialog:
    
    ```bash
    drawio -x -f xml --layout '[{"layout":"elkLayered","config":{"elk.direction":"RIGHT"}}]' -o diagram.drawio diagram.drawio
    ```
    
    Each entry is `{"layout": <algorithm>, "config": { â€¦ }}`:
    
    - **Algorithms**: `elkLayered`, `elkTree`, `elkRadial`, `elkOrganic`, `elkStress`, `elkBox`.
    - **`config`**: keys starting with `elk.` are ELK options â€” e.g. `elk.direction` (`UP` / `DOWN` / `LEFT` / `RIGHT`), `elk.spacing.nodeNode`, `elk.layered.spacing.nodeNodeBetweenLayers`. The keys `edgeStyle` (e.g. `orthogonal`) and `corners` (e.g. `rounded`) control connector rendering.
    
    ### Orthogonal edge routing
    
    `--layout libavoid` routes the **edges** orthogonally around the shapes (the editor's *Arrange â–¸ Layout â–¸ Orthogonal Routing*) without moving any vertex â€” the complement of the node layouts above. Use it as an in-place pass on hand-positioned XML whose connectors cross shapes:
    
    ```bash
    drawio -x -f xml --layout libavoid -o diagram.drawio diagram.drawio
    ```
    
    Skip it after a flow/tree preset â€” those already route their edges.
    
    **When to use it:** author the graph structure as XML without worrying about coordinates, then apply `verticalFlow` / `horizontalFlow` for flow-style diagrams or `organic` for networks. Mermaid-authored diagrams are already laid out â€” don't add `--layout`.
    
    ## Mermaid syntax reference
    
    When authoring Mermaid, fetch and follow the shared Mermaid reference (all supported diagram types plus flowchart styling â€” `style`, `classDef`, `linkStyle`):
    
    https://raw.githubusercontent.com/jgraph/drawio-mcp/main/shared/mermaid-reference.md
    
    Match the language of the diagram labels to the user's language.
    
    ## Choosing the output format
    
    Check the user's request for a format preference. Examples:
    
    - `/drawio:drawio create a flowchart` â†’ Mermaid â†’ `flowchart.drawio`
    - `/drawio:drawio png flowchart for login` â†’ Mermaid â†’ `login-flow.drawio.png`
    - `/drawio:drawio svg: ER diagram` â†’ Mermaid â†’ `er-diagram.drawio.svg`
    - `/drawio:drawio pdf AWS architecture overview` â†’ XML (needs AWS shapes) â†’ `architecture-overview.drawio.pdf`
    - `/drawio:drawio url flowchart for user login` â†’ opens browser at `app.diagrams.net` with the diagram, keeps `login-flow.drawio` locally
    
    If no format is mentioned, just produce the `.drawio` file and open it in draw.io. The user can always ask to export later.
    
    ### Supported export formats
    
    | Format | Embed XML | Notes |
    |--------|-----------|-------|
    | `png` | Yes (`-e`) | Viewable everywhere, editable in draw.io |
    | `svg` | Yes (`-e`) | Scalable, editable in draw.io |
    | `pdf` | Yes (`-e`) | Printable, editable in draw.io |
    | `jpg` | No | Lossy, no embedded XML support |
    
    PNG, SVG, and PDF all support `--embed-diagram` â€” the exported file contains the full diagram XML, so opening it in draw.io recovers the editable diagram.
    
    ## Browser URL output
    
    When the user requests `url` format, generate a draw.io URL that opens the diagram directly in the browser editor at `app.diagrams.net` â€” no draw.io Desktop required to *view* it. (Mermaid-authored diagrams still need the desktop CLI to convert to `.drawio` first; if no CLI is available, author the diagram as XML and build the URL from that.)
    
    ### How it works
    
    1. The `.drawio` file is written to disk as usual (gives the user a persistent local copy they can re-edit)
    2. The XML is compressed with Node.js's built-in `zlib` and base64-encoded
    3. The result is embedded in a `https://app.diagrams.net/#create=...` URL
    4. The URL is opened in the default browser
    
    This uses only Node.js built-in modules (`zlib`, `child_process`) â€” no external dependencies.
    
    ### URL generation
    
    Run this `node -e` one-liner to read the `.drawio` file and print the URL (replace `DIAGRAM.drawio` with the actual filename):
    
    ```bash
    URL=$(node -e '
    const fs = require("fs");
    const zlib = require("zlib");
    const xml = fs.readFileSync(process.argv[1], "utf8");
    const compressed = zlib.deflateRawSync(encodeURIComponent(xml)).toString("base64");
    const payload = encodeURIComponent(JSON.stringify({ type: "xml", compressed: true, data: compressed }));
    console.log("https://app.diagrams.net/?grid=0&pv=0&border=10&edit=_blank#create=" + payload);
    ' "DIAGRAM.drawio")
    ```
    
    The URL format matches the MCP Tool Server. Node.js's `zlib.deflateRawSync` and `pako.deflateRaw` both implement RFC 1951 and produce identical output, so URLs from either source are interchangeable.
    
    ### Opening the URL
    
    | Environment | Command |
    |-------------|---------|
    | macOS | `open "$URL"` |
    | Linux (native) | `xdg-open "$URL"` |
    | WSL2 | Write a temp `.url` file, open via `cmd.exe` (see below) |
    | Windows (native) | Write a temp `.url` file, open via `start` (see below) |
    
    **Why the `.url` workaround on Windows/WSL2?** `cmd.exe`'s `start` command treats `&` as a command separator and strips everything after `#` in URLs. The diagram payload lives in the `#create=...` fragment, so passing the URL directly causes it to be silently lost. A `.url` shortcut file preserves the URL intact.
    
    **macOS / Linux example:**
    
    ```bash
    open "$URL"      # macOS
    xdg-open "$URL"  # Linux
    ```
    
    **WSL2 example:**
    
    ```bash
    TMPFILE=$(mktemp --suffix=.url)
    printf '[InternetShortcut]\r\nURL=%s\r\n' "$URL" > "$TMPFILE"
    cmd.exe /c start "" "$(wslpath -w "$TMPFILE")"
    ```
    
    **Windows (native) example:**
    
    Do **not** build the `.url` file with `echo URL=%URL%`. The generated URL contains `&` characters (`?grid=0&pv=0&...`) that `cmd.exe` treats as command separators, so the shortcut is written truncated and the diagram payload is lost â€” the exact failure the `.url` file is meant to prevent. Let Node write the file directly (it already holds the URL string) and open only the resulting path, which never contains `&`:
    
    ```bash
    TMPFILE=$(node -e '
    const fs = require("fs");
    const os = require("os");
    const path = require("path");
    const p = path.join(os.tmpdir(), "drawio.url");
    fs.writeFileSync(p, "[InternetShortcut]\r\nURL=" + process.argv[1] + "\r\n");
    process.stdout.write(p);
    ' "$URL")
    cmd.exe /c start "" "$TMPFILE"
    ```
    
    ### After opening
    
    Print the URL so the user can copy or share it, and confirm the local file path:
    
    ```
    Opened in browser: <URL>
    Local file: DIAGRAM.drawio
    ```
    
    The `.drawio` file stays on disk so the user can re-edit it later, attach it elsewhere, or export it to an image format on demand.
    
    ### URL length
    
    The URL embeds the full compressed diagram in its hash fragment. Very large diagrams may hit browser URL length limits (typically ~32Kâ€“2MB depending on the browser). For complex diagrams that exceed the limit, fall back to writing the `.drawio` file and opening it locally.
    
    ## draw.io CLI
    
    The draw.io desktop app includes a command-line interface used for **converting Mermaid** to `.drawio`, applying **ELK layouts** (`--layout`), and **exporting** to PNG/SVG/PDF. All three require the desktop app to be installed.
    
    ### Locating the CLI
    
    First, detect the environment, then locate the CLI accordingly. On Windows (native and WSL2) the installer does **not** put draw.io on PATH and the install directory is user-selectable, so work through the whole fallback chain before concluding the CLI is absent.
    
    #### WSL2 (Windows Subsystem for Linux)
    
    WSL2 is detected when `/proc/version` contains `microsoft` or `WSL`:
    
    ```bash
    grep -qi microsoft /proc/version 2>/dev/null && echo "WSL2"
    ```
    
    On WSL2, use the Windows draw.io Desktop executable via `/mnt/c/...`:
    
    ```bash
    DRAWIO_CMD="/mnt/c/Program Files/draw.io/draw.io.exe"
    ```
    
    Double-quote the path so the space in `Program Files` is treated as part of the path. Do **not** wrap it in backticks â€” in bash, backticks are command substitution, which would try to *execute* the binary at locate-time instead of storing its path.
    
    If draw.io is installed in a non-default location, check common alternatives:
    
    ```bash
    # Default install path
    "/mnt/c/Program Files/draw.io/draw.io.exe"
    
    # Per-user install (if the above does not exist)
    "/mnt/c/Users/$WIN_USER/AppData/Local/Programs/draw.io/draw.io.exe"
    ```
    
    If neither exists, the install is on another drive or in a custom directory â€” scan the other mounted drives, then ask the registry:
    
    ```bash
    # Program Files on the other drives (D:, E:, â€¦)
    ls /mnt/*/Program\ Files/draw.io/draw.io.exe 2>/dev/null | head -1
    
    # Registry â€” the installer records the directory the user picked
    for KEY in 'HKCU\Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Uninstall' \
               'HKLM\Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Uninstall' \
               'HKLM\Software\WOW6432Node\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Uninstall'; do
      WIN_DIR=$(reg.exe query "$KEY" /s /v InstallLocation 2>/dev/null |
                grep -i 'REG_SZ.*draw\.io' | head -1 |
                sed 's/.*REG_SZ[[:space:]]*//' | tr -d '\r')
      [ -n "$WIN_DIR" ] && DRAWIO_CMD="$(wslpath -u "${WIN_DIR%\\}")/draw.io.exe" && break
    done
    ```
    
    #### macOS
    
    ```bash
    /Applications/draw.io.app/Contents/MacOS/draw.io
    ```
    
    #### Linux (native)
    
    ```bash
    drawio   # typically on PATH via snap/apt/flatpak
    ```
    
    Use `which drawio` to confirm it is on PATH.
    
    #### Windows (native, non-WSL2)
    
    Check, in order â€” a missing PATH entry or a non-`C:` install drive is normal, not a sign that draw.io is missing:
    
    1. **PATH**: `where.exe draw.io` (note the dot â€” the executable is `draw.io.exe`, not `drawio.exe`)
    2. **Default install paths**: `C:\Program Files\draw.io\draw.io.exe`, then the per-user install `%LOCALAPPDATA%\Programs\draw.io\draw.io.exe`
    3. **The registry** uninstall keys, which record the directory the user picked
    4. **The other drives**: `Program Files\draw.io\draw.io.exe` on `D:`, `E:`, â€¦
    
    Save this as `find-drawio.ps1` to run the whole chain at once â€” it prints the first executable it finds, and nothing at all if draw.io really is not installed:
    
    ```powershell
    $c = @()
    $c += (where.exe draw.io 2>$null)
    $c += "$env:ProgramFiles\draw.io\draw.io.exe", "$env:LOCALAPPDATA\Programs\draw.io\draw.io.exe"
    $c += Get-ItemProperty 'HKCU:\Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Uninstall\*',
                           'HKLM:\Software\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Uninstall\*',
                           'HKLM:\Software\WOW6432Node\Microsoft\Windows\CurrentVersion\Uninstall\*' -ErrorAction SilentlyContinue |
          Where-Object { $_.DisplayName -like '*draw.io*' } |
          ForEach-Object { if ($_.InstallLocation) { Join-Path $_.InstallLocation 'draw.io.exe' } else { ($_.DisplayIcon -split ',')[0] } }
    $c += (Get-PSDrive -PSProvider FileSystem).Root | ForEach-Object { Join-Path $_ 'Program Files\draw.io\draw.io.exe' }
    $c | Where-Object { $_ -and (Test-Path $_) } | Select-Object -First 1
    ```
    
    From a bash or cmd shell, run it with:
    
    ```bash
    powershell.exe -NoProfile -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -File find-drawio.ps1
    ```
    
    Quote the resulting path on every call â€” it almost always contains spaces:
    
    ```
    & "D:\Program Files\draw.io\draw.io.exe" -x -f xml -o diagram.drawio diagram.mmd
    ```
    
    Only once every step comes up empty should you treat the CLI as absent and fall back to XML authoring with `.drawio` / `url` output. When you do find it outside PATH, mention to the user that adding that folder (e.g. `D:\Program Files\draw.io`) to PATH makes it discoverable next time.
    
    ### Convert / layout / export commands
    
    **Convert Mermaid to `.drawio`:**
    
    ```bash
    drawio -x -f xml -o diagram.drawio diagram.mmd
    ```
    
    **Apply an ELK layout to XML** (see [ELK layout for XML](#elk-layout-for-xml)):
    
    ```bash
    drawio -x -f xml --layout verticalFlow -o diagram.drawio diagram.drawio
    ```
    
    **Export to an image format:**
    
    ```bash
    drawio -x -f <format> -e -b 10 -o "<output>" "<input.drawio>"
    ```
    
    **WSL2 export example:**
    
    ```bash
    "/mnt/c/Program Files/draw.io/draw.io.exe" -x -f png -e -b 10 -o "diagram.drawio.png" "diagram.drawio"
    ```
    
    Key flags:
    - `-x` / `--export`: export mode (also used for Mermaid conversion and layout passes)
    - `-f` / `--format`: output format (`xml`, png, svg, pdf, jpg) â€” use `xml` to produce a `.drawio` from Mermaid or a layout pass
    - `--layout`: run a layout before writing the output â€” an ELK preset name, the `libavoid` edge-routing pass, or a custom-layout JSON array
    - `--mermaid-image 1`: convert Mermaid to a single static SVG image cell (the Mermaid source stays on the cell for re-editing) instead of an editable diagram â€” only when the user explicitly asks for a non-editable image cell
    - `-e` / `--embed-diagram`: embed diagram XML in the output (PNG, SVG, PDF only)
    - `-o` / `--output`: output file path
    - `-b` / `--border`: border width around diagram (default: 0)
    - `-t` / `--transparent`: transparent background (PNG only)
    - `-s` / `--scale`: scale the diagram size
    - `--width` / `--height`: fit into specified dimensions (preserves aspect ratio)
    - `-a` / `--all-pages`: export all pages (PDF only)
    - `-p` / `--page-index`: select a specific page (1-based)
    - `--disable-gpu`: skip GPU initialisation â€” add it when a command dies with `GPU process isn't usable` (Windows remote-desktop sessions, VMs, CI runners); no convert, layout, or export path needs the GPU
    
    ### Opening the result
    
    | Environment | Command |
    |-------------|---------|
    | macOS | `open <file>` |
    | Linux (native) | `xdg-open <file>` |
    | WSL2 | `cmd.exe /c start "" "$(wslpath -w <file>)"` |
    | Windows | `start <file>` |
    
    **WSL2 notes:**
    - `wslpath -w <file>` converts a WSL2 path (e.g. `/home/user/diagram.drawio`) to a Windows path (e.g. `C:\Users\...`). This is required because `cmd.exe` cannot resolve `/mnt/c/...` style paths.
    - The empty string `""` after `start` is required to prevent `start` from interpreting the filename as a window title.
    
    **WSL2 example:**
    
    ```bash
    cmd.exe /c start "" "$(wslpath -w diagram.drawio)"
    ```
    
    ## File naming
    
    - Use a descriptive filename based on the diagram content (e.g., `login-flow`, `database-schema`)
    - Use lowercase with hyphens for multi-word names
    - When authoring Mermaid, write it to a matching `.mmd` file, convert to `.drawio`, then delete the `.mmd` â€” the `.drawio` is the artifact
    - For export, use double extensions: `name.drawio.png`, `name.drawio.svg`, `name.drawio.pdf` â€” this signals the file contains embedded diagram XML
    - After a successful export, delete the intermediate `.drawio` file â€” the exported file contains the full diagram
    - For `url` mode, keep the `.drawio` file (no double extension) â€” the URL is a view/edit handle and the local file is the persistent copy
    
    ## XML format
    
    A `.drawio` file is native mxGraphModel XML. When authoring as XML, generate it directly; Mermaid is converted to this same format by the CLI (`-f xml`), so both authoring routes end up as a native `.drawio`.
    
    ### Basic structure
    
    Every diagram must have this structure:
    
    ```xml
    <mxGraphModel adaptiveColors="auto">
      <root>
        <mxCell id="0"/>
        <mxCell id="1" parent="0"/>
        <!-- Diagram cells go here with parent="1" -->
      </root>
    </mxGraphModel>
    ```
    
    - Cell `id="0"` is the root layer
    - Cell `id="1"` is the default parent layer
    - All diagram elements use `parent="1"` unless using multiple layers
    - **Cells inside a container** use `parent="<container_id>"` and coordinates relative to that container
    - **Edges belong to the innermost container that holds BOTH endpoints** â€” same container (at any nesting depth) â†’ that container's id; one end outside every container â†’ `parent="1"`. An auto-layout reads an edge's coordinates in its parent's frame, so an edge parked further out than its endpoints is laid out in the wrong place
    
    (The example above uses an XML comment only to point out where cells go â€” never emit comments in real output; see [XML well-formedness](#critical-xml-well-formedness).)
    
    ## XML reference
    
    For the complete draw.io XML reference including common styles, edge routing, containers, layers, tags, metadata, dark mode colors, and XML well-formedness rules, fetch and follow the instructions at:
    https://raw.githubusercontent.com/jgraph/drawio-mcp/main/shared/xml-reference.md
    
    ## Troubleshooting
    
    | Problem | Cause | Solution |
    |---------|-------|----------|
    | draw.io CLI not found | Desktop app not installed or not on PATH | Author as XML and deliver a `.drawio` file or `url` (Mermaid conversion, ELK layout, and image export all need the desktop app). Tell the user they can install the draw.io desktop app to enable those |
    | Mermaid â†’ PNG export crashes | Direct `.mmd` â†’ PNG with `-e` is broken in current draw.io Desktop (embedded-XML step) | Use the two-step path: convert Mermaid to `.drawio` first (`-f xml`), then export the `.drawio` to PNG â€” the intermediate file embeds correctly |
    | Blank diagram from Mermaid | Misspelled type keyword, or a syntax error (bad node ID, unquoted label) | Check the [Mermaid reference](#mermaid-syntax-reference); the first non-directive line's keyword selects the diagram type |
    | Layout does nothing / errors | Unknown preset name, custom JSON not an array, or a desktop build too old for `--layout` / `.mmd` input | Use a preset from [Layout presets](#layout-presets) or a JSON array starting with `[`; on an old desktop build, author as XML with explicit positions and tell the user updating draw.io Desktop enables Mermaid conversion and layouts |
    | Export produces empty/corrupt file | Invalid XML (e.g. double hyphens in comments, unescaped special characters) | Validate XML well-formedness before writing; see the XML well-formedness section below |
    | Diagram opens but looks blank | Missing root cells `id="0"` and `id="1"` | Ensure the basic mxGraphModel structure is complete |
    | Edges not rendering | Edge mxCell is self-closing (no child mxGeometry element) | Every edge must have `<mxGeometry relative="1" as="geometry" />` as a child element |
    | File won't open after export | Incorrect file path or missing file association | Print the absolute file path so the user can open it manually |
    | `GPU process isn't usable. Goodbye.` during convert/layout/export | Electron cannot start a GPU process â€” typical on Windows remote-desktop sessions, VMs, and CI runners. The `os_crypt` / `Failed to decrypt` lines printed before it are unrelated noise | Retry the same command with `--disable-gpu` added; draw.io Desktop's own `--disable-acceleration` switch has the same effect |
    | Browser opens with empty diagram in `url` mode | `cmd.exe` stripped the `#create=...` fragment | Use the `.url` temp-file workaround on Windows/WSL2 (see [Opening the URL](#opening-the-url)) â€” never pass the URL directly to `cmd.exe /c start` |
    | URL is too long for the browser | Very large diagram exceeds browser URL length limit | Fall back to writing the `.drawio` file and opening it locally |
    
    ## CRITICAL: XML well-formedness
    
    - **NEVER include ANY XML comments (`<!-- -->`) in the output.** XML comments are strictly forbidden â€” they waste tokens, can cause parse errors, and serve no purpose in diagram XML.
    - Escape special characters in attribute values: `&amp;`, `&lt;`, `&gt;`, `&quot;`
    - Always use unique `id` values for each `mxCell`

    powershell.exe -NoProfile -ExecutionPolicy Bypass -File find-drawio.ps1
    ```
    
    Quote the resulting path on every call — it almost always contains spaces:
    
    ```
    & "D:\Program Files\draw.io\draw.io.exe" -x -f xml -o diagram.drawio diagram.mmd
    ```
    
    Only once every step comes up empty should you treat the CLI as absent and fall back to XML authoring with `.drawio` / `url` output. When you do find it outside PATH, mention to the user that adding that folder (e.g. `D:\Program Files\draw.io`) to PATH makes it discoverable next time.
    
    ### Convert / layout / export commands
    
    **Convert Mermaid to `.drawio`:**
    
    ```bash
    drawio -x -f xml -o diagram.drawio diagram.mmd
    ```
    
    **Apply an ELK layout to XML** (see [ELK layout for XML](#elk-layout-for-xml)):
    
    ```bash
    drawio -x -f xml --layout verticalFlow -o diagram.drawio diagram.drawio
    ```
    
    **Export to an image format:**
    
    ```bash
    drawio -x -f <format> -e -b 10 -o "<output>" "<input.drawio>"
    ```
    
    **WSL2 export example:**
    
    ```bash
    "/mnt/c/Program Files/draw.io/draw.io.exe" -x -f png -e -b 10 -o "diagram.drawio.png" "diagram.drawio"
    ```
    
    Key flags:
    - `-x` / `--export`: export mode (also used for Mermaid conversion and layout passes)
    - `-f` / `--format`: output format (`xml`, png, svg, pdf, jpg) — use `xml` to produce a `.drawio` from Mermaid or a layout pass
    - `--layout`: run a layout before writing the output — an ELK preset name, the `libavoid` edge-routing pass, or a custom-layout JSON array
    - `--mermaid-image 1`: convert Mermaid to a single static SVG image cell (the Mermaid source stays on the cell for re-editing) instead of an editable diagram — only when the user explicitly asks for a non-editable image cell
    - `-e` / `--embed-diagram`: embed diagram XML in the output (PNG, SVG, PDF only)
    - `-o` / `--output`: output file path
    - `-b` / `--border`: border width around diagram (default: 0)
    - `-t` / `--transparent`: transparent background (PNG only)
    - `-s` / `--scale`: scale the diagram size
    - `--width` / `--height`: fit into specified dimensions (preserves aspect ratio)

    1. Executive Introduction
    1.1 What is the Internal Operations Tracking System?
    Think of a large, high-performing airport operations centre. Dozens of people must do precise jobs at the exact right moment: planes must be fuelled, baggage loaded, flight paths verified, passengers boarded, and runways cleared. If baggage handlers are delayed, the pilot cannot take off. If the air traffic controller does not know who is at which gate, chaos ensues. The Airport Director does not load bags or steer planes, but needs an executive radar screen showing every flight, any safety delays, and on-time performance across all terminals.
    The CIRIL Internal Operations Tracking System (IOTS) is that exact flight operations control centre, built specifically for CIRIL—India’s premier commercial real estate alliance network.
    CIRIL brings together certified commercial real estate advisory firms across major metropolitan zones (North, South, East, West, Central). Corporate clients—major multinational IT companies, banks, and retail giants—need commercial offices, warehouses, and land across different cities. When a client in Mumbai needs a 50,000 sq ft office in Bengaluru, the Mumbai team originates the requirement (Lead Giver), CIRIL Headquarters coordinates and routes it to the vetted Bengaluru team (Lead Receiver), the Bengaluru team inspects properties, prepares institutional proposals, and closes the lease. Upon closing, brokerage fees are collected and split (30% to Lead Giver, 70% to Lead Receiver), and CIRIL receives its 7.5% corporate royalty.
    1.2 Why Does CIRIL Need This System?
    To keep this nationwide commercial machinery operating smoothly, CIRIL requires an Internal Operations Tracking System that guarantees:
    Every employee knows exactly what task they need to do today, when it is due, and what materials they need.
    Every manager knows who is working, who is overloaded, who is on leave, and which tasks are falling behind before deadlines are missed.
    The Chairman and Executive Leadership have complete 360-degree radar visibility over daily operational health, team output, location integrity, financial milestones, and outstanding exceptions.
    Outstanding employee dedication is tracked objectively and rewarded transparently through monthly and quarterly excellence recognitions.
    1.3 The Three Foundational System Layers
    The entire IOTS platform is structured into three foundational layers that work in complete harmony:
    
    Layer 1 – Authentication, Geolocation & Security Layer: No self-registration. Accounts are securely provisioned by authorised administrators. Everyone logs in through the same corporate portal. The system automatically inspects credentials, verifies the physical GPS location against authorised office boundaries, records an immutable audit log, and launches the user into their customised workspace. If an employee logs in from home or a client site, the system transparently tags the session as “Remote / Off-Site” without alarm.
    
    Layer 2 – Management, Operations & Execution Layer: Managers allocate work, balance workloads, review submissions, and resolve blockers. Execution staff focus on delivering tasks with high quality. Tasks follow a closed-loop lifecycle—no task can simply expire. When overdue, the system triggers active manager intervention. Managerial rejection enters a structured revision cycle preserving all historical versions.
    
    Layer 3 – Intelligence, Analytics & Reward Layer: Dashboards eliminate vanity metrics. Every graph answers a vital operational question. Predictive risk alerts highlight early warning signals before a missed deadline or client escalation occurs. Automated reporting engines generate daily digests, weekly team scorecards, and monthly executive summaries. The Performance & Reward System automatically evaluates delivery quality and rewards monthly excellence and 3-consecutive-month high performers with prestigious quarterly awards.
    
    2. Organizational Roles & Responsibilities
    2.1 The 11 Organisational Roles at a Glance
    The IOTS platform defines 11 distinct operational roles, ensuring complete clarity of ownership:

    Role Title
    Primary Scope
    Access Band

    Chairman
    Enterprise oversight, commercial pulse, escalations, strategic interventions, award sign-offs
    Higher-Level (Executive Interface)

    2.2 Detailed Role Breakdown
    2.2.1 Chairman
    Analogy: The Captain on the bridge of a naval ship. The Captain does not grease engines or peel vegetables, but continuously watches the global radar and steps in when high-risk hazards appear.
    Scope: Entire Enterprise (National HQ, All Geographic Zones, All Functional Departments).
    Key Responsibilities:
    Receives complete bird’s-eye operational intelligence across the entire alliance.
    Monitors high-level commercial deal flow and royalty collections (7.5% royalty health).
    Inspects company-wide attendance and location audit summaries, tracking remote/unknown location trends.
    Reviews the Executive “Requires Attention” Cockpit: overdue task clusters, repeated task reassignments, and unresolved departmental blockers.
    Reviews Quarterly Reward & Recognition Standings and personally signs off on the 3-consecutive-month excellence commendations.
    Can publish enterprise-wide Chairman Announcements.
    Cannot reset routine employee passwords or perform everyday task reassignment.
    2.2.2 Super Admin
    Analogy: The Chief Architect and Master Key Holder of a skyscraper. They configure master security doors, define who can enter which floor, and oversee system safety.
    System Parity Note: Super Admin and IT System Analyst have equal platform privileges. The Super Admin focuses on administrative governance; the IT Analyst focuses on technical infrastructure.
    Key Responsibilities:
    Manages master organisational configurations, department definitions, and role definitions.
    Configures Role-Based Access Control (RBAC) permission sets.
    Holds authority to deactivate or alter high-level accounts.
    Inspects full, immutable system audit logs.
    Manages data export covenants and compliance frameworks.
    2.2.3 Admin
    Analogy: The Facility Operations Manager of a corporate campus. They issue employee ID cards, set office opening hours, maintain the company directory, and announce building updates.
    Key Responsibilities:
    Structured User Provisioning: Manually creates internal user profiles (Employee ID, Designation, Department, Office Location, System Role, Reporting Manager). There is no public registration.
    Location & Geofence Perimeter Management: Configures approved office GPS coordinates and allowable radius boundaries.
    Attendance Auditing: Reviews location exceptions, off-site attendance flags, and unexcused late arrivals.
    Global Task Oversight: Monitors organisational work queues to identify abandoned tasks or perform archival cleanups.
    Company Announcements: Drafts and publishes official communications to specific teams or the entire organisation.
    2.2.4 IT System Analyst
    Analogy: The Chief Flight Engineer monitoring engine temperatures, avionics, electrical bus lines, and fuel injection systems.
    Possesses equal system privileges with Super Admin. Tailored for real-time technical monitoring and diagnostic intervention.
    Key Responsibilities:
    Application & Service Health: Monitors server uptime, database response latency, and frontend performance.
    External API Integrations: Tracks status and rate-limits of external services (BigDataCloud geocoding, mail servers, storage).
    Authentication & Security Auditing: Analyses anomalous login attempts, sudden location jumps, and session timeouts.
    Technical Incident Response: Logs and resolves platform bugs, database deadlocks, or webhook delivery failures.
    Deployment Tracking: Logs system version updates, environment variable changes, and schema migrations.
    Cannot arbitrarily approve business decisions (e.g., cannot approve commercial fee splits or employee annual leave).
    2.2.5 General Manager (CIRIL Staff: Ipsheeta)
    Analogy: The Managing Director of Operations who translates the Chairman’s strategy into smooth business coordination across legal, financial, and departmental divisions.
    Key Responsibilities (derived from Ipsheeta’s KRA):
    Board Coordination & Governance: Coordinates with the Board of Directors, prepares board meeting agendas and minutes, tracks execution of board decisions.
    Accounts & Finance Coordination: Collaborates with Accounts to track fee invoices, royalty remittance status (7.5%), and operational expenses.
    Audit & Statutory Compliance: Coordinates with external auditors and legal teams for timely compliance filings, NDAs, and corporate documentation.
    Conference & Event Management: Oversees planning and execution of the Annual Conference and Zonal Conferences.
    Membership Expansion Oversight: Conducts high-level virtual presentations to prospective member firms.
    Cross-Functional Escalation Resolution: Acts as the senior escalation clearinghouse for inter-department or inter-member disputes.
    2.2.6 Senior Manager
    Analogy: The Division Superintendent who oversees multiple project foremen, ensuring all teams move at the same speed.
    Key Responsibilities:
    Cross-Team Workload Balancing: Monitors workload distribution across operational, BD, and creative teams.
    SLA & Deadline Risk Mitigation: Identifies tasks at risk of missing client or member deadlines across multiple verticals.
    Manager Approval Queue: Evaluates high-impact decisions escalated by Lead Managers.
    Performance Comparison: Analyses comparative productivity trends between teams.
    2.2.7 Lead Manager (CIRIL Staff: Manasa)
    Analogy: The Operations Crew Chief on the ground. They know every team member by name, assign daily work, inspect finished deliverables, and ensure nothing falls behind.
    Key Responsibilities (derived from Key Tasks List):
    Member Office Deal & Royalty Coordination: Tracks concluded commercial transactions across member firms, verifying accurate 7.5% royalty and 30/70 fee splits.
    Vertical Calls Oversight: Manages and coordinates weekly vertical review calls (Retail, Logistics & Warehousing, Land, Hotel).
    Listing & Portal Management: Coordinates property listing uploads to commercial portals, ensuring timely distribution of leads.
    Market Report Data Collection: Coordinates with member brokerages to gather micro-market transaction data for quarterly research.
    Quarterly Statistics & Ratings: Prepares and distributes quarterly member performance scorecards and lead exchange matrices.
    Vendor Coordination: Manages printing vendors for market reports, stationery, event nameplates, and promotional materials.
    Day-to-Day Team Command: Monitors live employee availability, assigns tasks, conducts QA reviews, approves leaves, and intervenes on overdue tasks.
    2.2.8 Executive
    Focused on executing specific operational work with zero distractions. Executes assigned tasks, records progress, logs time, uploads supporting evidence, raises blocker tickets, and incorporates manager feedback in revision cycles.
    2.2.9 Business Development Executive (CIRIL Staff: Bhavani)
    Key Responsibilities (derived from Key Tasks List):
    Daily cold calls and structured email outreach to prospective commercial brokerage firms.
    Researches and compiles verified broker contacts from RERA portals, commercial directories, and Google.
    Operates Upturnly lead generation portal and 99acres commercial portal.
    Executes professional WhatsApp campaigns introducing CIRIL’s network.
    Maintains a rigorous follow-up queue, logging every call outcome and meeting schedule.
    2.2.10 Social Media Manager (CIRIL Staff: Harshitha)
    Note: Harshitha operates a multi-role profile—she serves as the Social Media Manager for brand campaigns while simultaneously acting as an Executive handling Office Vertical client calls and proofreading quarterly market reports.
    Key Responsibilities:
    End-to-End Social Media Management across LinkedIn, Instagram, YouTube, and corporate channels.
    Market Report Proofreading: Validates quarterly research publications for accuracy and brand alignment.
    Office Vertical Calls Support: Coordinates zone-wise Office Vertical calls and client updates.
    Conference Presentation Collaterals: Collaborates with the Graphic Designer for keynote slides and digital archives.
    Content Pipeline Tracking: Drives content items through Brief → Drafting → Internal Review → Changes Requested → Approved → Published.
    2.2.11 Graphic Designer
    Transforms raw data, presentations, and branding concepts into polished, institutional-grade visual assets. Delivers iterative design drafts with strict versioning (v1.0, v1.1, v2.0). Designs conference collaterals, ID badges, banners, and formats quarterly market research reports.
    2.3 Multi-Role Capability & Workspace Context Switching
    In lean organisations like CIRIL, professionals frequently hold dual responsibilities. The IOTS platform handles this elegantly:
    Unified Authentication: The user logs in once with their master corporate credentials.
    Account Profile Evaluation: The system detects all authorised system roles.
    Default Landing: The user lands on their primary role dashboard.
    Instant Context Switcher: A prominent dropdown in the global navigation bar allows the user to switch between authorised workspaces in a single click.
    Session & Audit Segregation: Every audit log entry captures both the employee’s identity and the active role context under which the action was performed.
    
    3. Task Lifecycle, Execution & Audit Architecture
    3.1 The 5 Core Reporting States
    Tasks move through five macro-level reporting states visible to executive leadership and analytics engines:
    State
    Type
    Definition
    ASSIGNED
    Active / Queued
    Formally created by a Manager with deliverables, priority tier, and deadline; assigned to an employee.
    PENDING
    Active / In-Flight
    Employee has acknowledged the assignment and work is underway within standard operational limits.
    OVERDUE
    Exception
    Target deadline has elapsed without approved completion. Requires immediate managerial intervention.
    REASSIGNED
    Active Transition
    Task transferred from one team member to another. NOT a terminal state—resumes active execution immediately under new owner.
    COMPLETED
    Terminal
    Deliverables thoroughly reviewed and officially approved by the designated Manager. Historical records sealed.
    3.2 The 7 Operational Micro-States
    During active execution, tasks pass through granular operational micro-states:
    Micro-State
    Indicator
    Meaning
    In Progress
    Blue Solid
    Employee is actively working, logging time, or drafting deliverables.
    Blocked
    Red Striped
    Employee has encountered a roadblock and raised an Issue Ticket. Work is paused.
    Waiting for Review
    Amber Pulse
    Employee has uploaded deliverables and formally clicked “Submit for Review”.
    Under Review / QA
    Purple Solid
    Manager has opened the submission and is reviewing deliverables against quality criteria.
    Changes Requested
    Orange Solid
    Manager returned the submission with itemised revision notes.
    Issue Resolved
    Teal Solid
    The reported blocker has been cleared, enabling work to resume.
    Done / Closed
    Emerald Solid
    Manager confirmed all acceptance criteria met; task closed and archived.
    3.3 Employee Work Execution & Activity Logging
    A critical design principle: States represent where the task sits in the governance pipeline. Work Activities are chronological records created during that state. An employee can log ten activities during a single “In Progress” state.
    The four structured work activities:
    Progress Percentage Updates: Slider or numeric input (0% to 100%) with a mandatory brief summary of work accomplished.
    Work Notes & Context Memos: Freeform text capturing conversations, client remarks, phone call outcomes, or research findings.
    Time-Spent / Effort Logging: Hours and minutes recorded against the task, enabling delivery velocity calculations.
    Supporting Deliverables & Evidence: Direct file attachments (spreadsheets, PDFs, presentations, images) with automatic version indexing.
    3.4 Overdue Handling: Active Managerial Intervention
    In IOTS, Overdue is an actionable operational alarm, not a passive badge. When the clock crosses the deadline:
    The task status transitions automatically to OVERDUE.
    A priority alert is dispatched to the Lead Manager’s Manager Action Centre.
    The system presents mandatory Overdue Intervention options:
    
    Option 1: Grant a deadline extension with a mandatory business justification (e.g., “Awaiting registered lease copy from Mumbai sub-registrar”).
    Option 2: Reassign to an available team member after inspecting workload capacity.
    Option 3: Escalate to Senior Manager for cross-departmental resource deployment.
    Option 4: Scope reduction / direct guidance to the employee.
    A Manager cannot simply click “+1 Day” without entering a formal justification. This justification is permanently recorded in the audit trail.
    3.5 The Reassignment Protocol
    Reassignment is a controlled workflow, not a casual drag-and-drop:
    Manager selects a task and clicks “Reassign Task”.
    The system displays the Team Bandwidth Radar, showing current task loads of all team members.
    Manager selects a standardised reassignment reason: Employee Capacity/Overload, Medical/Approved Leave, Skillset Required, or Client Vertical Shift.
    Manager writes a mandatory handover note detailing what was completed and what remains.
    Indelible audit log entry records the transfer. The previous employee is relieved of deadline liability, and the new employee receives an urgent handover alert.
    3.6 The Quality Review & Correction Cycle
    Managerial review is never a blunt “Approve” or “Reject”. Rejection initiates a collaborative correction cycle:
    Employee submits deliverables (files + notes). Task moves to “Waiting for Review”.
    Manager reviews against quality criteria.
    If approved: Task marked “Completed”. Performance points awarded.
    If changes required: Manager returns the submission with itemised deficiency notes. Task re-enters “In Progress (Revision 2)”.
    Employee implements corrections and resubmits with a revision changelog.
    Manager conducts final QA approval. Task formally closed.
    3.7 The Immutable Audit History Component
    Every critical object in IOTS (Tasks, User Accounts, Geofences, Leave Requests, Royalty Settlements) contains an embedded Audit History Ledger.
    Immutable Ledger Rules:
    Append-Only: Audit records can never be updated, overwritten, or deleted by any user, including Super Admins.
    Deterministic Attribution: Every entry records Actor ID, Actor Name, Active Role, Timestamp (ISO-8601 to the millisecond), Origin IP, and Geolocation Context.
    Before & After Delta: Records precisely what changed (e.g., Priority: Normal → Critical).
    Source Distinction: Differentiates between User-Initiated Actions and System-Automated Actions.
    Each audit entry displays: Actor, Role, Timestamp, Action, Object/Resource, Previous Value, New Value, Reason/Comment, and Source (User Action / System Action). The component is strictly read-only and supports filtering by date, actor, action type, and resource.
    
    4. Attendance, Geolocation Engine & Security Auditing
    4.1 Real-Time Availability States
    At any point during the workday, an employee’s live availability status is visible across the system:
    Status
    Indicator
    Definition & Impact
    Available
    Green Solid
    Logged in, at desk or approved workstation, ready to accept new assignments.
    Busy
    Red Solid
    Actively executing a high-priority task. Avoid urgent interruptions.
    In Meeting
    Blue Solid
    Participating in a team standup, zone-wise vertical call, or client briefing.
    On Break
    Amber Solid
    Stepped away for lunch, tea, or personal rest. Work-timer paused.
    In Training
    Indigo Solid
    Undergoing onboarding, compliance modules, or software training.
    Do Not Disturb
    Purple Solid
    Deep focus mode (e.g., Designer formatting an annual report, BDE on focused cold-calling).
    Working Remotely
    Cyan Solid
    Logged in from home or approved off-site location with verified geolocation context.
    On Leave
    Gray Solid
    Formally approved leave. Excluded from daily active task allocations.
    Absent
    Crimson Solid
    Unexcused failure to log in within grace period. Triggers HR attendance flag.
    Offline
    Slate Solid
    Logged off at end of shift.
    4.2 The Geolocation Engine & Office Perimeter Geofencing
    When an employee enters their credentials on the unified login screen, the system executes a three-step security handshake:
    Credential Validation: Verifies Employee ID and Password against the internal directory.
    Hardware GPS Query: Requests the browser’s Hardware GPS API for latitude and longitude coordinates.
    Reverse Geocoding & Perimeter Check: Resolves the coordinates to a human-readable address (via BigDataCloud API), then compares the distance against the assigned office’s approved geofence perimeter using the Haversine formula.
    
    If the employee is within the approved office radius (e.g., 100 metres), the session is tagged “Office / On-Premise” with a green badge. If outside the office perimeter, the session is tagged “Remote / Off-Site” with a calm amber pill and an optional reason memo—the system does NOT block them or treat them as a criminal.
    4.3 Handling Remote & Unknown Locations: Dignity Over Fear
    The IOTS philosophy:
    No frightening error screens. If an employee is outside the office perimeter, they are not blocked.
    Contextual Tagging: A calm message states “We notice you are signing in from outside your designated office perimeter. Your session has been marked as Remote / Off-Site.”
    Optional Reason Memo: The employee can enter a quick note (e.g., “Working from home with prior approval”).
    Manager Notification: The Lead Manager sees a subtle orange pin next to the employee’s name on their live team radar.
    4.4 Attendance & Work Hours Computation
    IOTS eliminates paper registers and manual time tracking:
    Check-In Timestamp: Captured automatically upon first successful login of the day.
    Check-Out Timestamp: Captured when the user clicks “Check-Out & Logoff”.
    Net Work Hours: Active productive time = (Check-Out – Check-In) – Total Break Durations.
    Standard Shift: 9:30 AM to 6:30 PM (9.0 Gross Hours / 8.0 Net Productive Hours).
    Grace Period: 15 minutes. Arrivals up to 9:45 AM marked on-time.
    Late Arrivals: Logins after 9:45 AM flagged. Three unexcused late arrivals per month trigger automated HR and Lead Manager notification.
    4.5 Employee Self-Service Request Portal
    Employees manage their work-life schedules through five request categories:
    Leave Requests: Casual Leave (CL), Sick Leave (SL), Earned/Privilege Leave (PL), and Compensatory Off.
    Short Permission Requests: 1 to 2 hours for doctor appointments, personal emergencies, or government office visits.
    Scheduled Remote Work Requests: Prior approval to work from home for specific days.
    Task Clarification / Guidance Requests: Urgent 15-minute sync with the Lead Manager.
    Operational Resource Requests: Hardware, software licences, commercial portal credits, or conference stationery.
    4.6 Security Auditing & Chairman Anomaly Cockpit
    The Chairman, Super Admin, and IT System Analyst access the Master Security & Location Audit Centre. Automated anomaly detection covers:
    Impossible Travel Velocity: Login in Hyderabad, then same account logs in from Dubai 45 minutes later. System suspends the second session and dispatches urgent alerts.
    Ghost Logins & Session Hijacking: Active sessions are bound to cryptographically signed browser tokens. Copied cookies on a different browser are immediately invalidated.
    Unapproved Remote Clusters: More than 5 consecutive off-site days without approved leave triggers an administrative flag.
    Concurrent Multi-Device Logins: Simultaneous sessions from different IP subnets raise a security investigation event.
    Every login event is preserved with its reverse-geocoded address, GPS coordinates, public IP address, ISP provider, and browser user-agent.
    
    5. CIRIL Commercial Operations & Revenue Architecture
    5.1 The CIRIL Syndication Model
    CIRIL operates as an apex clearinghouse and national alliance of certified commercial real estate advisory firms (Member Companies). The platform provides national umbrella coverage, standardised institutional proposal templates, trusted dispute resolution, proprietary market intelligence, and automated fee distribution.
    5.2 The 4-Tier Geographic Hierarchy
    Country → Zones (North, South, East, West, Central) → States (Regulatory Jurisdictions) → Cities (Metropolitan Commercial Centres) → Member Companies → Advisory Associates.
    5.3 The 6-Phase End-to-End Transaction Lifecycle
    Mandate Origination (Lead Giver Stage): A Member Company in City A secures a mandate from an enterprise client. The broker logs the mandate with comprehensive parameters—space category, vertical type, required area, target budget, and technical specifications.
    Central Clearance & Destination Handoff: CIRIL HQ verifies the mandate and routes it to an accredited Member Company in the destination city (Lead Receiver). The recipient must formally Accept or Reject within 24 hours.
    Sourcing & Institutional Proposals: The Lead Receiver sources inventory and generates standardised presentations—a First-Cut Proposal (comparison matrix) and a Detailed Institutional Proposal (exhaustive building report).
    Site Inspections & 7-Stage Negotiation: The deal advances through Basic Prospecting → Site Inspection → Shortlisting → Commercial Offer → Term Sheet → Legal Drafting → Agreement Execution.
    Deal Conclusion & Financial Lock: Upon execution of the registered lease deed or sale deed, confirmed transaction metrics are locked.
    Fee Apportionment & Royalty Settlement: The system calculates gross brokerage, applies the member split, extracts CIRIL’s 7.5% corporate royalty, and dispatches official conclusion statements.
    5.4 Fee Structure & 7.5% Royalty Engine
    Base Brokerage Calculation:
    Commercial Lease: Gross Brokerage = 1 Month (30 Days) Rental Value.
    Outright Sale / Acquisition: Gross Brokerage = 1.00% of Total Transaction Consideration.
    
    Inter-Member Split:
    Lead Giver (LG) Share: 30% of Gross Brokerage (for origination and client relationship management).
    Lead Receiver (LR) Share: 70% of Gross Brokerage (for local sourcing, inspections, negotiations, and closing).
    
    CIRIL Corporate Royalty = 7.5% of Total Gross Brokerage, deducted proportionally from both LG (7.5% of LG share) and LR (7.5% of LR share).
    
    5.4.1 Financial Simulation: Grade-A Office Lease
    Financial Metric
    Value
    Deal Parameters
    50,000 sq ft @ ₹80 / sq ft / month
    Gross Transaction Value
    ₹40,00,000 / month rent
    Total Gross Brokerage Pool
    ₹40,00,000 (1 Month Rental)
    Lead Giver Share (30%)
    ₹12,00,000
    Lead Receiver Share (70%)
    ₹28,00,000
    Royalty by LG (7.5%)
    ₹90,000
    Royalty by LR (7.5%)
    ₹2,10,000
    Net CIRIL Corporate Royalty
    ₹3,00,000
    Net Retained by LG
    ₹11,10,000
    Net Retained by LR
    ₹25,90,000
    5.5 Proprietary Market Intelligence Engine
    Rental Trends Benchmark: Tracks micro-market rental movements, vacancy absorptions, and effective lease rates.
    Landmark Deals Archive: Catalogs major multi-thousand sq ft leases across BFSI, IT/ITeS, and manufacturing sectors.
    Upcoming Supply Pipeline: Verified registry of under-construction commercial towers and industrial parks.
    Landlord & Tenant Data Mining: Tracks upcoming enterprise lease expirations for early client renewal and mandate origination.
    
    6. Intelligence, Analytics & Automated Reporting
    6.1 The 3 Reporting Rhythms
    Rhythm
    Cadence
    Focus
    Audience
    Daily
    Generated 18:30 IST
    Tasks completed today, overdue items, unresolved blockers, tomorrow’s priorities
    Employees & Lead Managers
    Weekly
    Monday 09:00 IST
    SLA on-time delivery rate, workload rebalancing, team capacity trends
    Senior Managers & General Manager
    Monthly
    1st of Month
    Departmental target fulfilment, royalty settlements, reward system standings
    Chairman & Board of Directors
    6.2 The 6 Analytics Centres
    Centre 1: Performance & Velocity Analytics
    Answers: How fast, how well, and how consistently are teams delivering work?
    Task Completion Rate: Completed Tasks / Total Assigned Tasks × 100.
    On-Time SLA Delivery Rate: Tasks Completed On or Before Deadline / Total Completed Tasks × 100.
    Average Turnaround Duration: Mean time from Assigned to Completed across task categories.
    Rework & Revision Index: Frequency of tasks returned with Changes Requested.
    Centre 2: Task & Workload Distribution Analytics
    Answers: Is work distributed fairly, or are specific individuals becoming bottlenecks?
    Team Workload Capacity Radar: Active task commitments vs. standard 8-hour daily capacity.
    Concentration Analysis: Identifies if 80% of critical tasks are loaded onto 20% of staff.
    Task Pipeline Distribution: Real-time breakdown of tasks in each state.
    Centre 3: Attendance & Workforce Health Analytics
    Punctuality Index, attendance consistency, workplace ratio (On-Premise vs. Remote), and availability patterns.
    Centre 4: Trend & Historical Pattern Analysis
    Time-horizon comparative analysis with toggles: Today vs. Yesterday, This Week vs. Last Week, Last 30 Days vs. Prior 30 Days, and Quarter-to-Date vs. Prior Quarter.
    Centre 5: Exception & Risk Analysis (3-Tier Engine)
    Risk Tier
    Criteria
    System Response
    Tier 1: Low
    Task due in <4h with <30% progress; first-time punctuality flag; single revision requested
    Informational alert in daily digest
    Tier 2: Medium
    Task overdue by 4–24 hours; task reassigned 2+ times; employee capacity exceeds 110%; blocker open >8 hours
    Amber flash in Lead Manager Action Centre; demands active resolution
    Tier 3: High / Critical
    Task overdue >48 hours; unresolved blocker halting commercial deal; repeated unexcused absences; impossible travel anomaly
    Escalates to Senior Manager and Chairman; requires logged intervention
    Centre 6: Predictive Operational Signals
    Deterministic operational warnings based on mathematical thresholds (not fabricated certainty):
    SLA Breach Warning: If 2 hours remain but average completion duration for the task type is 4 hours and progress is only 15%.
    Burnout Early-Warning: If an employee logs >10 net work hours for 4 consecutive days while maintaining >100% capacity.
    Vendor Bottleneck Warning: Historical vendor delivery delays automatically prompting milestone schedule adjustments.
    6.3 Role-Scoped Automated Report Delivery
    To the Chairman: 2-page Executive Radar—enterprise operational health, royalty totals, critical escalations, and 3-month award candidates.
    To General Manager & Senior Managers: Cross-departmental workload matrices, SLA trends, overdue resolution audits.
    To Lead Managers: Actionable daily checklists—tasks needing QA review, pending leave approvals, overdue interventions.
    To Execution Staff: Personal monthly scorecards—completed tasks, on-time delivery %, time tracked, and reward milestone progress.
    
    7. Employee Performance, Reward & Recognition System
    7.1 The Philosophy of Objective Recognition
    In conventional organisations, bonuses and awards are decided through subjective impressions, recency bias, or office politics. This damages morale. The CIRIL IOTS Reward System establishes complete objectivity and transparency. Because every task, delivery timestamp, review cycle, and attendance record is captured accurately, recognition is calculated through transparent mathematical scoring. Every employee can see their live performance score and reward trajectory daily.
    7.2 The 100-Point Performance Scoring Formula
    At the end of each calendar month, the IOTS Intelligence Engine computes an Overall Monthly Performance Score on a 100-point scale:
    
    Component
    Max Points
    Measurement
    Task Delivery & Velocity
    35
    35 × (Completed Tasks / Total Assigned Tasks)
    On-Time SLA Achievement
    30
    30 × (Tasks Completed On or Before Deadline / Total Completed Tasks)
    First-Time Quality & Revision Index
    15
    15 × (1 – Tasks Returned for Revision / Total Submissions)
    Punctuality & Location Integrity
    10
    10 points for 100% on-time arrivals, zero unexcused anomalies. −2 per unexcused late arrival beyond grace.
    Initiative & Blocker Resolution
    10
    Evaluated by Lead Manager during monthly reviews for proactive problem-solving.
    7.3 Performance Tiers & Monthly Rewards
    Tier
    Score Range
    Reward & Recognition
    Gold Tier
    90 – 100
    “Star Performer of the Month” digital badge, cash spot bonus / voucher, spotlight on announcement board, counts as 1 month toward 3-month award.
    Silver Tier
    80 – 89
    “High Achiever” commendation in team standup, full quarterly bonus eligibility.
    Bronze Tier
    70 – 79
    Meets baseline corporate expectations.
    Needs Focus
    < 70
    Triggers constructive 1-on-1 coaching with Lead Manager; automated review of workload or training gaps.
    7.4 The 3-Consecutive-Month Chairman’s Quarterly Excellence Award
    The Rule of Unwavering Consistency: Any employee can have a lucky month. True institutional excellence is demonstrated through sustained consistency.
    Continuous Qualification: An employee must achieve Gold Tier (score ≥ 90) in three consecutive calendar months (e.g., October, November, December).
    The Reset Covenant: If an employee achieves Gold in Month 1 and Month 2, but dips to Silver (85) in Month 3, the consecutive streak counter resets to zero.
    Chairman Cockpit Shortlist: Upon completing the third consecutive Gold month, the system automatically generates a Chairman Quarterly Honours Dossier presenting the 90-day activity summary.
    Executive Commendation: The Chairman reviews the dossier and signs off on the award.
    
    Quarterly Honours Package:
    Substantial Cash Incentive dispatched alongside the quarterly payroll.
    Formal Chairman’s Trophy & Framed Commendation, presented publicly during CIRIL’s Annual or Zonal Conference.
    Permanent Gold Star Profile Medallion displayed alongside the employee’s name across all system modules.
    Fast-Track Career Progression: Serves as the primary prerequisite for promotions.
    7.5 Role-Specific KPI Customisation
    To ensure fairness across diverse functional departments, the scoring engine tailors metrics by role:
    BDE (Bhavani): Outreach volume (calls, emails, WhatsApp sequences), prospect pipeline conversions, follow-up adherence.
    Social Media Manager (Harshitha): Content calendar adherence, market report proofreading timeliness, campaign delivery.
    Graphic Designer: Creative turnaround speed, version iteration efficiency (≤2 revision cycles), conference collateral print readiness.
    Lead Manager (Manasa): Team SLA adherence, royalty calculation accuracy, workload balance health.
    7.6 Anti-Burnout Safeguards
    No Midnight Heroics Required: Points are capped at standard shift durations. Working 14-hour days does not grant extra points.
    Transparent Dispute Resolution: If an employee feels their score was unfairly affected by external blockers, they can submit a 1-click Score Review Request to the Senior Manager.
    Open Self-Inspection: Employees can click on any score component to view the exact math and underlying records.

    9. Enterprise Dashboard Blueprints
    Every authenticated screen shares an identical application shell: organisation/system identity, current user and role, branch/location, availability indicator, global notifications, global search, breadcrumbs, left navigation sidebar, and a responsive 12-column content grid.
    9.1 Chairman Dashboard (Apex Executive Cockpit)
    Navigation: Overview, Operational Performance, Global Work, Escalations, People Overview, Analytics & Insights, Reports, Announcements, Audit Summary, Profile & Security.
    Top-row enterprise health radar cards: Enterprise Operational Health %, Active Mandates & Deals, Critical Escalations, Workforce Availability.
    Requires Attention Panel: Overdue tasks >48 hours, repeated reassignments, blocked deal closures.
    Organisational Pulse: 30-day task completion velocity, on-time SLA trend, quarterly alliance growth.
    Global Work Monitor: Task, Owner, Team, Priority, Due Date, Status, Risk/Attention, Last Activity.
    Executive interventions require secondary confirmation to ensure strategic weight.
    9.2 Super Admin Dashboard (Platform Governance)
    Navigation: Platform Settings, RBAC & Permissions, Master Verticals, Data Compliance, Full Audit Ledger.
    Master Metrics: Active System Accounts, Geofence Perimeters, Data Storage Utilisation, Global System Status.
    RBAC Policy Editor: Visual permission matrix toggling capabilities across user designations.
    9.3 Admin Dashboard (Routine Administration)
    Navigation: User Directory, Location & Geofencing, Global Task Oversight, Announcements, Attendance Audit.
    User Provisioning: Structured multi-step creation—Identity, Role, Department, Reporting Manager.
    Geofence Manager: Map view configuring office latitude, longitude, and radius buffers.
    Announcement Studio: Rich-text editor with audience selectors (All Staff, Specific Roles, Specific Zones).
    9.4 IT System Analyst Dashboard (Technical Reliability)
    Navigation: System Health, API & Microservices, Security Diagnostics, Technical Incidents, Change Logs.
    Service Uptime Monitors: Real-time ping latency and status cards for Reverse Geocoding API, Auth Gateway, PostgreSQL Cluster, and Cloud File Buckets.
    Authentication Anomalies Radar and One-Click Session Killer with mandatory incident logging.
    9.5 General Manager Dashboard (Ipsheeta – Operations & Governance)
    Navigation: Business Overview, Board Coordination, Accounts & Royalties, Legal Compliance, Expansion Pipeline.
    Governance Centre: Board meeting agenda builder, minute tracker, action item registry.
    Royalty Reconciliation Ledger: Open fee invoices, 7.5% royalty clearances, overdue payments from member brokerages.
    Legal Compliance Vault: Executed membership agreements, NDAs, and statutory filing deadlines.
    9.6 Senior Manager Dashboard (Multi-Team Operations)
    Navigation: Multi-Team Overview, Cross-Team Workload, SLA Delivery, Escalation Centre, Senior Approvals.
    Cross-Team Balance Radar: Side-by-side capacity metres for Operations, BD, and Creative teams.
    Escalation Clearinghouse and Senior Approval Queue for major deadline adjustments (>3 days).
    9.7 Lead Manager Dashboard (Manasa – Team Command)
    Navigation: Team Command, Task Manager, Live Status, Workload, Approvals/QA, Leave Requests, Blocker Centre.
    Live Team Radar: Every team member with real-time availability, active task title, and hours logged today.
    Task Assignment Engine: Modal to create tasks with priority, deliverables, SLA deadline, and automated bandwidth warning.
    Overdue Intervention Centre: Action cards providing 1-click Extend (with reason), Reassign (with context), or Escalate.
    QA Review Workbench: Split view with employee files on left and manager review controls on right.
    9.8 Executive Dashboard (Core Work Execution)
    Navigation: My Workspace, Active Tasks, Work Activity, Blockers, Requests, Attendance, My Performance.
    Today’s Focus Banner highlighting the single highest-priority task with a prominent “Continue Work” button.
    Work Activity Logger: Quick modal to increment progress, record notes, log time, and attach files.
    Report Blocker Button: 1-click form to pause work and alert the Lead Manager.
    9.9 BDE Dashboard (Bhavani – Expansion)
    Navigation: BD Command, Outreach Quota, Prospect Database, Portals (Upturnly / 99acres), Follow-up Queue.
    Daily Quota Progress Bar: Calls completed (e.g., 34/50), emails dispatched, WhatsApp intros sent.
    Prospect Database Table: Contacts filtered by city, source (RERA, Google), and qualification status.
    Follow-up Calendar: Chronological callbacks scheduled for today with 1-click call logging.
    9.10 Social Media Manager Dashboard (Harshitha – Campaigns)
    Navigation: Content Pipeline, Content Calendar, Campaign Hub, Report Proofreading, Creative Requests.
    Visual Content Kanban: 6-column pipeline tracking: Brief → Drafting → Internal Review → Changes Requested → Approved → Published.
    Content Calendar View: Monthly grid displaying scheduled releases across LinkedIn, Instagram, and YouTube.
    9.11 Graphic Designer Dashboard (Creative Workbench)
    Navigation: Creative Queue, Active Briefs, Version Asset Vault, Revision Centre, Conference Collaterals.
    Version Asset Manager: File vault displaying iterative versions (v1.0, v1.1, v2.0) with visual thumbnails, author notes, and download links.
    Revision Workspace: Side-by-side view highlighting requested changes from the Social Media Manager or Lead Manager.
    
    10. Core System Principles & Operational Guarantees
    Complex Underneath, Simple on Top: The underlying data model tracks sub-metre GPS coordinates, millisecond audit events, and intricate revenue splits. But the user interface remains clean, calm, and self-explanatory.
    Zero Passive Task Drift: A task can never simply “expire” into oblivion. When a deadline passes, the system immediately demands manager review and structured resolution.
    Immutable Accountability (Audit by Design): Every major operational action writes an indelible entry into the audit trail. Audit logs cannot be altered or deleted by any user, including Super Admins.
    Equity and Transparency: Performance is evaluated using verifiable operational metrics rather than subjective opinions. Every employee can view their own scorecards in real time.
    Security with Dignity: Location verification protects corporate integrity and confirms workplace safety, not punitive surveillance. Remote and off-site work is seamlessly accommodated.
    Unified Login, Multi-Role Flexibility: Single corporate login for all 11 roles. Employees with dual responsibilities switch workspace contexts instantly without re-authentication.
    Every Chart Answers a Business Question: No decorative charts. Every visualisation in the Intelligence & Analytical Layer is designed to answer a specific operational question.
    Constructive Correction, Never Punitive Termination: Managerial rejection of deliverables does not kill the task. It initiates a structured, traceable revision cycle with collaborative feedback.
    
    
    — END OF DOCUMENT —
